    "oldString": "identifying \\emph{coupling alignment} as the decisive factor (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
    "newString": "empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nPreliminary single-seed cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} and Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} reveals consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects: Mistral shows 5/6 task wins; at 14B, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, identifying \\emph{coupling alignment} as the decisive factor (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).\n\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nWe identify \\emph{coupling alignment} as the decisive factor in training sparsity predictors: only objectives aligned with the final output distribution discover composable masks, while per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines despite supervised training.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) are the proximate quality driver---gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% at 30\\%, renders compensation networks redundant, and delivers $7.3\\times$ smaller cross-seed $\\sigma$ under uniform allocation.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer to downstream tasks: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K), revealing a task-type-dependent tradeoff.\nPreliminary cross-architecture evaluation confirms consistent PPL improvements (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks); at 14B scale, reasoning declines despite PPL gains, suggesting scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes investigating per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, adapting the framework to N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+ models where the PPL-downstream gap may shift.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines despite supervised training.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) are the proximate quality driver---gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% at 30\\%, renders compensation networks redundant, and delivers $7.3\\times$ smaller cross-seed $\\sigma$ under uniform allocation.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer to downstream tasks: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K), revealing a task-type-dependent tradeoff.\nPreliminary cross-architecture evaluation confirms consistent PPL improvements (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks); at 14B scale, reasoning declines despite PPL gains, suggesting scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes investigating per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, adapting the framework to N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+ models where the PPL-downstream gap may shift.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "7cce405a-cde2-4301-8ece-3ef616af456a",

  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "At 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (8.81 vs.\\ 10.82) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).",
    "newString": "At 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (8.81 vs.\\ 10.82) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nPreliminary single-seed cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} and Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} reveals consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects: Mistral shows 5/6 task wins; at 14B, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, identifying \\emph{coupling alignment} as the decisive factor (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).\n\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 12,
        "oldLines": 7,
        "newStart": 12,
        "newLines": 7,
        "lines": [
          " A target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.",
          " ",
          " We instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.",
          "-At 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 19\\% (8.81 vs.\\ 10.82) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).",
          "+At 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).",
          " At 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.",
          " ",
          " Preliminary single-seed cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} and Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} reveals consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects: Mistral shows 5/6 task wins; at 14B, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp)."
        ]

    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/related_work.tex",
    "oldString": "This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.\nAll prior predictor-based methods use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.",
    "newString": "This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.\nIn MaskLLM's N:M setting, each mask group is optimized independently with a fixed within-group budget, so the KL objective's cross-neuron coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods. Under global allocation, every neuron competes against all others across all layers for a shared budget; this creates long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE fundamentally cannot capture, amplifying the gap between coupled and decomposed objectives.\nAll prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.\nWang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse} address the interaction between dynamic sparsity and quantization with zigzag-patterned weight layouts.\nOWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines non-uniform per-layer sparsity ratios based on activation outlier distribution for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision for intermediate coupling within each layer but without end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but the two methods differ in three fundamental ways.\nFirst, \\emph{sparsity type}: MaskLLM targets N:M semi-structured \\emph{weight} sparsity (static 2:4 patterns), while we target \\emph{activation} sparsity (dynamic per-token masks). Weight masks are fixed after training; our masks vary with each input token.\nSecond, \\emph{mask generation}: MaskLLM's masks are directly learnable parameters (one per weight group) selected via Gumbel-Softmax; we train a predictor network that generates masks at inference from hidden states for input-dependent decisions.\nThird, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups with no cross-group budget competition; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers. This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.\nAll prior predictor-based methods use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nOur contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON (Spontaneous Neurons;~\\citealp{xu2026spon}) learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers that produces input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity.\nSwitch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{MoE Routing and Gated Sparsity.}\nExpert Choice routing~\\cite{zhou2022expertchoice} lets each expert select its top-$k$ tokens under a fixed capacity budget, a global allocation perspective that parallels TEAL's neuron-level global top-$k$.\nD2D-MoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} converts dense SwiGLU layers into dynamic-$k$ MoE by exploiting the same activation sparsity we target, but restructures the network into discrete experts rather than predicting masks on the original architecture.\nMoNE~\\cite{cheng2025mone} applies neuron-granular top-$k$ selection within each MoE expert during pretraining for fine-grained conditional computation without additional routing parameters.\nGated architectures beyond SwiGLU (e.g., Griffin's RG-LRU~\\cite{de2024griffin}) produce analogous structured sparsity through their gating mechanisms; output-distribution prediction may therefore generalize beyond standard Transformer FFNs.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "c5f38800-f5ae-436e-b4fd-dd77409d112a",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "7c35a53b-05c5-4b25-8dac-930c6d29b33e",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "f02d9833-be0c-4682-b81e-b99604974bb7",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B:",
    "newString": "\\item Preliminary analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B:",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nPreliminary single-seed cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} and Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} reveals consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects: Mistral shows 5/6 task wins; at 14B, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).\n\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 23,
        "oldLines": 5,
        "newStart": 23,
        "newLines": 5,
        "lines": [
          " \\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).",
          " \\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
          " \\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
          "-\\item Analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).",
          "+\\item Preliminary analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).",
          " \\end{itemize}"
        ]
      }

We did not evaluate downstream task performance on C4; the OOD robustness of KL's downstream advantages remains unverified. Extending OOD evaluation to 70\\% sparsity is left to future work.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\\% sparsity achieves PPL 8.36 but MMLU already degrades; 30\\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity.\nTraining requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.\nOn Mistral-7B, KL wins 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\\% but GSM8K reverses (11.1\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 15.9\\%), consistent with GSM8K's sensitivity to the sparsity objective: reasoning tasks may require different optimization targets than knowledge and commonsense benchmarks; forward KL's mass-covering property may preserve output breadth at the expense of reasoning-critical pathways.\nThe 30\\% sparsity analysis is validated at 7--8B scale; at 14B, KL's MMLU decline ($-$2.79pp uniform, $-$8.1pp global) and GSM8K reversal ($-$20pp uniform; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 52.1\\%, 3-seed) reflect task-and-scale-dependent effects (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_arch}; Appendix~\\ref{app:14b}): at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K, so this reversal emerges only with sufficient base capacity. KL's high cross-seed GSM8K variance ($\\sigma$=5.2pp) suggests minor training perturbations disrupt reasoning-critical pathways that magnitude pruning preserves more robustly. Scaling to 70B remains untested.\nOut-of-distribution evaluation on C4 (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}) shows the KL advantage transfers but with a wider gap: C4 PPL at 30\\% is $1.74\\times$ dense vs.\\ $1.46\\times$ on in-domain WikiText-2, which indicates that the predictor partially overfits to training-domain activation patterns. Extending OOD evaluation to 70\\% sparsity is left to future work.\nThe target-swap ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}) confirms that mask quality drives inference-stage quality, with KL's coupled gradients necessary at the discovery stage. C1 demonstrates mask transferability but does not fully resolve whether gradient coupling has additional benefits beyond target discovery. The mechanism by which KL's coupled gradient discovers lower-perplexity mask patterns during training remains incompletely characterized; future work could trace per-layer mask divergence trajectories across training to identify when and where the patterns diverge.\nAt 30\\% sparsity, global allocation exhibits 9.5pp cross-seed MMLU standard deviation (3-seed); allocation strategy interacts with predictor initialization in task-specific ways. At 8B, this variance is MMLU-specific under global allocation, as ARC, WinoGrande, and GSM8K show stable performance across seeds ($\\sigma$$\\leq$0.7pp); at 14B, GSM8K variance emerges ($\\sigma$=5.2pp, above).\nWhile we motivate our work with inference speedup potential, actual wall-clock acceleration depends on sparse kernel implementations (e.g., Triton, cuSPARSE) and hardware-specific memory access patterns; this work focuses on prediction quality rather than systems optimization.\nFuture directions include N:M structured sparsity for hardware-native acceleration, scaling to 13B+ models, and training a single predictor that serves multiple sparsity targets simultaneously.\n",

          "-\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2026spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.",
          "+\\emph{Static} methods~\\cite{xu2025spon} learn a fixed bias vector per layer that does not depend on the input token.",
          " \\emph{Per-token} methods~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward,akhauri2024shadowllm,szatkowski2024d2dmoe} produce a unique mask for each input position, conditioning on the token's hidden state at each layer.",

        "oldStart": 139,
        "oldLines": 7,

          " \\emph{Intermediate} objectives (MSE on hidden states) capture local coupling within a single layer but not end-to-end output effects.",
          " \\emph{Coupled} objectives (KL on output distribution) propagate gradients through the full forward pass; each mask decision's gradient reflects its joint effect with all other masks on the final distribution.",
          " ",
          "-SPON~\\cite{xu2026spon} distills a per-layer bias via KL but cannot adapt to per-input neuron importance (static $\\times$ coupled).",

          " DejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} and FastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} achieve per-token granularity but use BCE, where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.",
          " D2DMoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} provides intermediate coupling (MSE on hidden states) but not end-to-end output effects.",
          " Our method combines per-token prediction with coupled KL training (Table~\\ref{tab:taxonomy}), propagating gradients through all downstream layers."

    "newString": "xu2025spon",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.\nWang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse} address the interaction between dynamic sparsity and quantization with zigzag-patterned weight layouts.\nOWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines non-uniform per-layer sparsity ratios based on activation outlier distribution for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision for intermediate coupling within each layer but without end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but the two methods differ in three fundamental ways.\nFirst, \\emph{sparsity type}: MaskLLM targets N:M semi-structured \\emph{weight} sparsity (static 2:4 patterns), while we target \\emph{activation} sparsity (dynamic per-token masks). Weight masks are fixed after training; our masks vary with each input token.\nSecond, \\emph{mask generation}: MaskLLM's masks are directly learnable parameters (one per weight group) selected via Gumbel-Softmax; we train a predictor network that generates masks at inference from hidden states for input-dependent decisions.\nThird, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups with no cross-group budget competition; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers. This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.\nIn MaskLLM's N:M setting, each mask group is optimized independently with a fixed within-group budget, so the KL objective's cross-neuron coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods. Under global allocation, every neuron competes against all others across all layers for a shared budget; this creates long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE fundamentally cannot capture, amplifying the gap between coupled and decomposed objectives.\nAll prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nOur contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON (Spontaneous Neurons;~\\citealp{xu2026spon}) learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers that produces input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity.\nSwitch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{MoE Routing and Gated Sparsity.}\nExpert Choice routing~\\cite{zhou2022expertchoice} lets each expert select its top-$k$ tokens under a fixed capacity budget, a global allocation perspective that parallels TEAL's neuron-level global top-$k$.\nD2D-MoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} converts dense SwiGLU layers into dynamic-$k$ MoE by exploiting the same activation sparsity we target, but restructures the network into discrete experts rather than predicting masks on the original architecture.\nMoNE~\\cite{cheng2025mone} applies neuron-granular top-$k$ selection within each MoE expert during pretraining for fine-grained conditional computation without additional routing parameters.\nGated architectures beyond SwiGLU (e.g., Griffin's RG-LRU~\\cite{de2024griffin}) produce analogous structured sparsity through their gating mechanisms; output-distribution prediction may therefore generalize beyond standard Transformer FFNs.\n",

    "originalFile": "\\section*{Ethics Statement}\n\nThis work proposes a training method for sparsity predictors in pretrained language models, evaluated on publicly available models (LLaMA-3.1-8B, Mistral-7B-v0.3, Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}) and standard benchmarks (WikiText-2, WikiText-103, C4, ARC, WinoGrande, HellaSwag, PIQA, MMLU, GSM8K).\nNo new data collection, annotation, or human subjects are involved.\nThe method does not alter model outputs beyond the sparsity-induced degradation we analyze; it introduces no new generation capabilities or safety risks beyond those inherent in the base models.\nCompute requirements are modest (1000 training steps, $\\sim$45 minutes on a single GPU for KL training), presenting minimal environmental concern relative to the pretrained models we evaluate on.\nWe release no model weights or user-facing systems; our contribution is analytical (understanding KL distillation for sparsity) rather than deployment-oriented.\n\n\\section*{Responsible NLP Research Checklist}\n\n\\paragraph{A. Limitations and Risks.}\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item[\\textbf{A1.}] Limitations are discussed in the Limitations section. Key limitations: evaluation limited to 7--14B models; perplexity is an imperfect proxy for downstream quality; wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not implemented here.\n\\item[\\textbf{A2.}] No foreseeable risks specific to this work beyond those inherent in the base language models. The method does not enable new capabilities or applications.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{B. Scientific Artifacts.}\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item[\\textbf{B1.}] Yes. All models (LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}, Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral}, Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25}), datasets (WikiText~\\cite{merity2017pointer}, C4~\\cite{raffel2020c4}), and methods (TEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal}, SPON~\\cite{xu2026spon}) are cited with original references.\n\\item[\\textbf{B2.}] LLaMA-3.1 is released under the Llama 3.1 Community License; Mistral-7B under Apache 2.0. WikiText and C4 are publicly available research datasets. Our use is consistent with their intended purposes.\n\\item[\\textbf{B3.}] N/A. We do not release new artifacts in this submission.\n\\item[\\textbf{B4.}] N/A. No personally identifiable information or offensive content is involved.\n\\item[\\textbf{B5.}] N/A. No new datasets created.\n\\item[\\textbf{B6.}] N/A. No crowdsourced data.\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{C. Computational Experiments.}\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item[\\textbf{C1.}] Yes. Model sizes (8B base, 75.9M predictor), training compute (1000 steps, batch 4, seq 2048), optimizer (AdamW~\\cite{loshchilov2019adamw}, lr=$10^{-3}$, cosine decay), and GPU (NVIDIA RTX PRO 6000 Blackwell Server Edition, 96\\,GB, for both training and latency measurement) are reported in \\S4.1 and Appendix A.\n\\item[\\textbf{C2.}] Yes. Hyperparameters are fully specified: bottleneck $d_b{=}128$, temperature schedule $\\tau: 1.0 \\to 0.1$, sparsity penalty parameters ($\\epsilon{=}0.02$, $\\lambda_{\\max}$), weight decay $5{\\times}10^{-2}$.\n\\item[\\textbf{C3.}] Yes. Three-seed evaluation reported (PPL std=0.07, CV$<$1\\%; Appendix E).\n\\item[\\textbf{C4.}] Yes. All evaluation uses established metrics (perplexity, accuracy) on standard benchmarks with standard few-shot protocols (ARC 25-shot, WinoGrande 5-shot, HellaSwag 10-shot, PIQA 0-shot, MMLU 5-shot, GSM8K 8-shot).\n\\end{itemize}\n\n\\paragraph{D. 158	\paragraph{Loss Function Isolation.}
159	Removing the penalty from BCE+Gumbel improves PPL (32.22 vs.\ 38.02), partly due to a sparsity gap (38.3\% actual vs.\ 50\% target), but remains $2.7\times$ worse than KL (11.79) and worse than vanilla BCE at matched 50\% (27.97).
160	BCE+Gumbel underperforms standard BCE because Gumbel's continuous relaxation conflicts with BCE's per-neuron binary classification: stochastic sampling degrades the binary target signal. In contrast, KL benefits from Gumbel by enabling end-to-end gradient flow through the mask. % exp_id: bce_gumbel_no_penalty_s42, bce_gumbel_lagrangian_isolation, eval_bce_v3_s42
161	We note that the loss and relaxation effects are not strictly additive: BCE+Gumbel (32.22) performs worse than plain BCE (27.97), suggesting a negative interaction between per-neuron objectives and stochastic relaxation, while KL benefits from Gumbel's exploration. This positive KL$\times$Gumbel interaction means the decomposition in Table~\ref{tab:ablation} is approximate.
162	The loss effect ($\Delta$PPL=20.4, i.e., $32.22{-}11.79$) is $5.1\times$ the relaxation effect ($\Delta$PPL=3.98).
163	
164	\paragraph{Compensation Redundancy.}
165	Adding compensation~\cite{gautam2026fastforward} (+67.2M params) to KL provides no benefit at either sparsity level (PPL 8.97 vs.\ 8.96 at 30\%, identical at 50\%; Appendix~\ref{app:comp_convergence}), as compensation networks designed for fixed-mask inference do not transfer to TEAL's dynamic allocation. % exp_id: kl_comp_30_s42, kl_comp_30_s456, kl_comp_50_s42, bce_comp_staged_s42
```

_source line 6102 · 2026-05-20T07:02:10.871Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_0174UScjChK4KVxf5TK651on`</summary>

```text
18	Second, \emph{mask generation}: MaskLLM's masks are directly learnable parameters (one per weight group) selected via Gumbel-Softmax; we train a predictor network that generates masks at inference from hidden states for input-dependent decisions.
19	Third, \emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups with no cross-group budget competition; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers. This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.
20	In MaskLLM's N:M setting, each mask group is optimized independently with a fixed within-group budget, so the KL objective's cross-neuron coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods. Under global allocation, every neuron competes against all others across all layers for a shared budget; this creates long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE fundamentally cannot capture, amplifying the gap between coupled and decomposed objectives.
21	All prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.
22	Our contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.
23	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "Third, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups with no cross-group budget competition; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers. This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.\nIn MaskLLM's N:M setting, each mask group is optimized independently with a fixed within-group budget, so the KL objective's cross-neuron coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods. Under global allocation, every neuron competes against all others across all layers for a shared budget; this creates long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE fundamentally cannot capture, amplifying the gap between coupled and decomposed objectives.",
    "newString": "Third, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups where KL coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers, creating long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE cannot capture and amplifying the coupled-vs-decomposed gap.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Related Work}\n\n\\paragraph{Training-Free Activation Sparsity.}\nTEAL~\\cite{liu2025teal} applies global top-$k$ selection by activation magnitude and achieves 40--50\\% sparsity with no training cost.\nCATS~\\cite{lee2024cats} adapts per-layer thresholds for SiLU/SwiGLU activations; R-Sparse~\\cite{zhang2025rsparse} predicts sparsity from rank-aware SVD projections; DIP~\\cite{federici2025dip} combines dynamic input pruning with cache-aware masking; SparseInfer~\\cite{shin2025sparseinfer} predicts activation sparsity patterns from attention outputs without additional training.\nWang et al.~\\cite{wang2025dynamicsparse} address the interaction between dynamic sparsity and quantization with zigzag-patterned weight layouts.\nOWL~\\cite{yin2024owl} determines non-uniform per-layer sparsity ratios based on activation outlier distribution for weight pruning.\nAll training-free methods determine masks from fixed statistics or input-independent criteria, while our predictor generates \\emph{input-dependent} masks trained to minimize output-distribution divergence.\n\n\\paragraph{Predictor-Based Contextual Sparsity.}\nDejaVu~\\cite{liu2023dejavu} introduced learned sparsity predictors trained via BCE to classify each neuron independently.\nShadowLLM~\\cite{akhauri2024shadowllm} uses attention-based routing with per-layer MSE supervision for intermediate coupling within each layer but without end-to-end gradient signal.\nFastForward~\\cite{gautam2026fastforward} adds a 67.2M-parameter compensation network using BCE for mask prediction, adding inference latency.\nSVD-CSP~\\cite{serbin2026svdcsp} compresses sparsity predictors via SVD decomposition for reduced predictor overhead.\nPowerInfer~\\cite{song2024powerinfer} exploits power-law activation frequencies for GPU--CPU scheduling.\nMaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} shares the principle of frozen weights + learnable masks + KL training, but the two methods differ in three fundamental ways.\nFirst, \\emph{sparsity type}: MaskLLM targets N:M semi-structured \\emph{weight} sparsity (static 2:4 patterns), while we target \\emph{activation} sparsity (dynamic per-token masks). Weight masks are fixed after training; our masks vary with each input token.\nSecond, \\emph{mask generation}: MaskLLM's masks are directly learnable parameters (one per weight group) selected via Gumbel-Softmax; we train a predictor network that generates masks at inference from hidden states for input-dependent decisions.\nThird, \\emph{allocation}: MaskLLM optimizes within fixed N:M groups with no cross-group budget competition; our masks are ranked under a global top-$k$ budget across all layers. This global competition creates the inter-neuron dependencies that motivate coupled training.\nIn MaskLLM's N:M setting, each mask group is optimized independently with a fixed within-group budget, so the KL objective's cross-neuron coupling operates only within small local neighborhoods. Under global allocation, every neuron competes against all others across all layers for a shared budget; this creates long-range dependencies that per-neuron BCE fundamentally cannot capture, amplifying the gap between coupled and decomposed objectives.\nAll prior predictor-based methods for activation sparsity use decomposed (BCE) or intermediate (MSE) objectives where each neuron's gradient is independent of other mask states.\nOur contribution is primarily empirical: we observe that a coupled KL objective propagates gradients through all downstream layers and quantify when this matters for global allocation quality.\n\n\\paragraph{Static Bias Correction.}\nSPON (Spontaneous Neurons;~\\citealp{xu2025spon}) learns input-independent bias vectors via closed-form KL minimization per layer, absorbed into weights at deployment.\nOur method is fundamentally different: a per-token contextual predictor trained end-to-end across all layers that produces input-dependent masks.\nSPON solves a static per-layer problem; we solve a global dynamic prediction problem. The two are weakly correlated (cosine similarity 0.196, $R^2$=0.038; \\S\\ref{sec:experiments}).\n\n\\paragraph{Activation Function Engineering.}\nReLU Strikes Back~\\cite{mirzadeh2024relu} replaces SiLU with ReLU for $>$90\\% natural sparsity; TurboSparse~\\cite{song2024turbosparse} and ProSparse~\\cite{song2025prosparse} engineer extreme sparsity through activation modifications; the Sparsing Law~\\cite{luo2024sparsinglaw} formalizes how activation density increases with training compute.\nMiniTron~\\cite{muralidharan2024minitron} combines structured pruning with distillation but modifies architecture permanently.\nAll require retraining or weight modification; our method is a plug-in predictor on \\emph{unmodified} SwiGLU models.\n\n\\paragraph{Weight Pruning, Distillation, and Routing.}\nWanda~\\cite{sun2024wanda}, SparseGPT~\\cite{frantar2023sparsegpt}, and SliceGPT~\\cite{ashkboos2024slicegpt} produce static weight masks via one-shot or structured pruning; Polar Sparsity~\\cite{shrestha2025polar} scales contextual sparsity to batched inference by shifting focus from MLP to attention head sparsity.\nSwitch Transformers~\\cite{fedus2022switch} and Mixture-of-Depths~\\cite{raposo2024mod} achieve conditional computation through learned routing.\nKL-based distillation~\\cite{kim2016sequencekd,sanh2019distilbert,gu2024minillm,agarwal2024gkd,ko2024distillm} trains compact students; SparseLoRA~\\cite{khaki2025sparselora} exploits contextual sparsity for fine-tuning.\nWe apply the KL principle to train a sparsity predictor on unmodified dense models, using Gumbel-Sigmoid sampling~\\cite{maddison2017concrete,louizos2018l0} for differentiable discrete mask optimization.\n\n\\paragraph{MoE Routing and Gated Sparsity.}\nExpert Choice routing~\\cite{zhou2022expertchoice} lets each expert select its top-$k$ tokens under a fixed capacity budget, a global allocation perspective that parallels TEAL's neuron-level global top-$k$.\nD2D-MoE~\\cite{szatkowski2024d2dmoe} converts dense SwiGLU layers into dynamic-$k$ MoE by exploiting the same activation sparsity we target, but restructures the network into discrete experts rather than predicting masks on the original architecture.\nMoNE~\\cite{cheng2025mone} applies neuron-granular top-$k$ selection within each MoE expert during pretraining for fine-grained conditional computation without additional routing parameters.\nGated architectures beyond SwiGLU (e.g., Griffin's RG-LRU~\\cite{de2024griffin}) produce analogous structured sparsity through their gating mechanisms; output-distribution prediction may therefore generalize beyond standard Transformer FFNs.\n",

_source line 6133 · 2026-05-20T07:03:03.853Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_01FB8yMWkxewjqHqJoiit3QC`</summary>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Our experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines despite supervised training.
5	A target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\% divergent from magnitude) are the proximate quality driver---gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.
6	A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\% at matched sparsity, renders compensation networks redundant, and delivers $7.3\times$ smaller cross-seed $\sigma$ under uniform allocation.
7	However, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer to downstream tasks: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K), revealing a task-type-dependent tradeoff.
8	Preliminary cross-architecture evaluation confirms consistent PPL improvements (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks); at 14B scale, reasoning declines despite PPL gains, suggesting scale-dependent effects.
9	Future work includes investigating per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, adapting the framework to N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+ models where the PPL-downstream gap may shift.
10	
```

</details>

    "oldString": "Our experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines despite supervised training.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) are the proximate quality driver---gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity, renders compensation networks redundant, and delivers $7.3\\times$ smaller cross-seed $\\sigma$ under uniform allocation.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer to downstream tasks: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K), revealing a task-type-dependent tradeoff.\nPreliminary cross-architecture evaluation confirms consistent PPL improvements (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks); at 14B scale, reasoning declines despite PPL gains, suggesting scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes investigating per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, adapting the framework to N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+ models where the PPL-downstream gap may shift.",
    "newString": "Our experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer MSE degrades below training-free baselines.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity and renders compensation networks redundant.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K); preliminary cross-architecture evaluation (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks) and scale analysis (14B: reasoning declines despite PPL gains) reveal task- and scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+.",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines despite supervised training.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) are the proximate quality driver---gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity, renders compensation networks redundant, and delivers $7.3\\times$ smaller cross-seed $\\sigma$ under uniform allocation.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer to downstream tasks: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K), revealing a task-type-dependent tradeoff.\nPreliminary cross-architecture evaluation confirms consistent PPL improvements (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks); at 14B scale, reasoning declines despite PPL gains, suggesting scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes investigating per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, adapting the framework to N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+ models where the PPL-downstream gap may shift.\n",

205	
206	\paragraph{Cross-Architecture and Scale.}
207	\label{sec:cross_arch}
208	On Mistral-7B-v0.3~\cite{jiang2023mistral}, KL outperforms TEAL on 5/6 tasks at 30\% (ARC +9.0pp, HellaSwag +8.8pp, WG +5.6pp; GSM8K reverses; Table~\ref{tab:mistral_downstream}). % exp_id: mistral_kl30_downstream, mistral_teal30_downstream
209	On Qwen-2.5-14B~\cite{qwen2024qwen25}, PPL generalizes ($1.52\times$ dense) but MMLU ($-$2.79pp) and GSM8K ($-$20pp, 3-seed) decline (Table~\ref{tab:14b}); at 14B TEAL retains 59\% of dense reasoning vs.\ KL's 37\%, suggesting scale-dependent interaction with task-specific circuits. % exp_id: qwen14b_kl30_uniform_4task, qwen14b_teal30_uniform
210	The full sparse pipeline adds 6.4\% latency overhead (Appendix~\ref{app:latency}); net acceleration requires sparse kernel support. % exp_id: latency_profiling
211

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer MSE degrades below training-free baselines.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity and renders compensation networks redundant.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K); preliminary cross-architecture evaluation (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks) and scale analysis (14B: reasoning declines despite PPL gains) reveal task- and scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes per-layer coupling mechanisms along the coupling-alignment continuum, N:M structured sparsity, and scaling to 70B+.\n",

[Director 指令] [Patch — R15 补充修复] 上一批遗漏 2 项，追加执行。

## 11. limitations.tex L3: PPL 8.36 → 10.76（CRITICAL 遗漏）

当前 L3: "40% sparsity achieves PPL 8.36 but MMLU already degrades"

问题：8.36 来自旧实验 d030_kl40_uniform (per_token eval mode)，与论文标准 teal_global 不一致。30% PPL=8.81 → 40% PPL=8.36 = regime 曲线非单调，审稿人必然质疑。

改为：
"40\% sparsity achieves PPL 10.76 but MMLU already degrades to 35.5\% (vs.\ 40.7\% at 30\%); 30\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity."

## 12. N1 wording 精修（Reviewer 建议更好的平衡点）

我上一批给的 N1 改法略 over-correct。Reviewer 建议保留 coupling alignment 作为分析框架，只降级发现式语气。更新如下：

- **conclusion.tex L4**: 不用我上一批给的版本，改用 Reviewer 版本：
"Our experiments demonstrate that \emph{coupling alignment}---optimizing a composable, output-level quantity---is necessary for sparsity prediction quality, a principle well-established in end-to-end learning but previously unverified for activation sparsity."

- **introduction.tex L25** contribution bullet 3: 不用我上一批的版本，改用：
"Empirical evidence that per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines, demonstrating the necessity of output-aligned training for composable masks (\S\ref{sec:method:taxonomy})."

- **method.tex Table 1 caption (L120)**: 加 "(organizational framework)" 后缀：
"...Our method occupies the Per-token $\times$ Coupled cell (organizational framework)."

- **abstract.tex L5**: 我上一批给的版本可以保留（"suggesting that alignment...matters more than gradient coupling alone"），与 Conclusion 的 "previously unverified" 定位一致。

如果上一批的改动已经做了，以本批为准覆盖 N1 相关的 3 处（Conclusion/Intro/Table caption）。limitations.tex 是新增项。

_source line 6187 · 2026-05-20T07:05:10.252Z_

<details><summary>↩ Tool result · `toolu_017B2LrhuWyWkemQYmUTK8vC`</summary>

```text
0	\section*{Limitations}
1	
2	Our primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\% sparsity achieves PPL 8.36 but MMLU already degrades; 30\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity.
3	Training requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.
4	On Mistral-7B, KL wins 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\% but GSM8K reverses (11.1\% vs.\ TEAL's 15.9\%), consistent with GSM8K's sensitivity to the sparsity objective: reasoning tasks may require different optimization targets than knowledge and commonsense benchmarks; forward KL's mass-covering property may preserve output breadth at the expense of reasoning-critical pathways.
```

</details>

```text
1	\section{Conclusion}
2	\label{sec:conclusion}
3	
4	Our experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer MSE degrades below training-free baselines.
5	A target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\% divergent from magnitude) drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.
6	A predictor ($<$1\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\% at matched sparsity and renders compensation networks redundant.
7	However, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K); preliminary cross-architecture evaluation (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks) and scale analysis (14B: reasoning declines despite PPL gains) reveal task- and scale-dependent effects.
8	Future work includes N:M structured sparsity and scaling to 70B+.
9	
```

21	Our contributions are:
22	\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]
23	\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\S\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).
24	\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\S\ref{sec:isocompute}).
25	\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\S\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).
26	\item Preliminary analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\S\ref{sec:scaling}).
27	\end{itemize}

    "newString": "40\\% sparsity achieves PPL 10.76 but MMLU already degrades to 35.5\\% (vs.\\ 40.7\\% at 30\\%); 30\\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity.",
    "originalFile": "\\section*{Limitations}\n\nOur primary evaluation uses WikiText-2 perplexity, and downstream benchmarks reveal that perplexity is a poor proxy for reasoning ability at moderate sparsity: 40\\% sparsity achieves PPL 8.36 but MMLU already degrades; 30\\% appears to be the lowest-degradation sparsity level for downstream fidelity.\nTraining requires 1000 steps on WikiText-103 ($\\sim$45 minutes on RTX PRO 6000 for KL, $\\sim$17 minutes for BCE), but a separate predictor must be trained for each sparsity target.\nOn Mistral-7B, KL wins 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\\% but GSM8K reverses (11.1\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 15.9\\%), consistent with GSM8K's sensitivity to the sparsity objective: reasoning tasks may require different optimization targets than knowledge and commonsense benchmarks; forward KL's mass-covering property may preserve output breadth at the expense of reasoning-critical pathways.\nThe 30\\% sparsity analysis is validated at 7--8B scale; at 14B, KL's MMLU decline ($-$2.79pp uniform, $-$8.1pp global) and GSM8K reversal ($-$20pp uniform; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 52.1\\%, 3-seed) reflect task-and-scale-dependent effects (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_arch}; Appendix~\\ref{app:14b}): at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K, so this reversal emerges only with sufficient base capacity. KL's high cross-seed GSM8K variance ($\\sigma$=5.2pp) suggests minor training perturbations disrupt reasoning-critical pathways that magnitude pruning preserves more robustly. Scaling to 70B remains untested.\nOut-of-distribution evaluation on C4 (Appendix~\\ref{app:ood}) shows the KL advantage transfers but with a wider gap: C4 PPL at 30\\% is $1.74\\times$ dense vs.\\ $1.46\\times$ on in-domain WikiText-2, which indicates that the predictor partially overfits to training-domain activation patterns. We did not evaluate downstream task performance on C4; the OOD robustness of KL's downstream advantages remains unverified. Extending OOD evaluation to 70\\% sparsity is left to future work.\nThe target-swap ablation (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}) confirms that mask quality drives inference-stage quality, with KL's coupled gradients necessary at the discovery stage. C1 demonstrates mask transferability but does not fully resolve whether gradient coupling has additional benefits beyond target discovery. The mechanism by which KL's coupled gradient discovers lower-perplexity mask patterns during training remains incompletely characterized; future work could trace per-layer mask divergence trajectories across training to identify when and where the patterns diverge.\nAt 30\\% sparsity, global allocation exhibits 9.5pp cross-seed MMLU standard deviation (3-seed); allocation strategy interacts with predictor initialization in task-specific ways. At 8B, this variance is MMLU-specific under global allocation, as ARC, WinoGrande, and GSM8K show stable performance across seeds ($\\sigma$$\\leq$0.7pp); at 14B, GSM8K variance emerges ($\\sigma$=5.2pp, above).\nWhile we motivate our work with inference speedup potential, actual wall-clock acceleration depends on sparse kernel implementations (e.g., Triton, cuSPARSE) and hardware-specific memory access patterns; this work focuses on prediction quality rather than systems optimization.\nFuture directions include N:M structured sparsity for hardware-native acceleration, scaling to 13B+ models, and training a single predictor that serves multiple sparsity targets simultaneously.\n",

          " On Mistral-7B, KL wins 5 of 6 downstream tasks at 30\\% but GSM8K reverses (11.1\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 15.9\\%), consistent with GSM8K's sensitivity to the sparsity objective: reasoning tasks may require different optimization targets than knowledge and commonsense benchmarks; forward KL's mass-covering property may preserve output breadth at the expense of reasoning-critical pathways.",
          " The 30\\% sparsity analysis is validated at 7--8B scale; at 14B, KL's MMLU decline ($-$2.79pp uniform, $-$8.1pp global) and GSM8K reversal ($-$20pp uniform; 32.1$\\pm$5.2\\% vs.\\ TEAL's 52.1\\%, 3-seed) reflect task-and-scale-dependent effects (\\S\\ref{sec:cross_arch}; Appendix~\\ref{app:14b}): at 8B all methods collapse to $<$9\\% GSM8K, so this reversal emerges only with sufficient base capacity. KL's high cross-seed GSM8K variance ($\\sigma$=5.2pp) suggests minor training perturbations disrupt reasoning-critical pathways that magnitude pruning preserves more robustly. Scaling to 70B remains untested."
        ]
      }

    "originalFile": "\\section{Conclusion}\n\\label{sec:conclusion}\n\nOur experiments suggest that alignment with the output distribution matters more than gradient coupling alone: only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, while per-layer MSE degrades below training-free baselines.\nA target-swap ablation confirms that KL-discovered mask patterns (46--68\\% divergent from magnitude) drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling is necessary for mask discovery but not at inference.\nA predictor ($<$1\\% of base) reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity and renders compensation networks redundant.\nHowever, PPL gains do not uniformly transfer: KL improves commonsense reasoning (4/4 tasks) but underperforms on knowledge-intensive benchmarks (MMLU, GSM8K); preliminary cross-architecture evaluation (Mistral-7B: 5/6 tasks) and scale analysis (14B: reasoning declines despite PPL gains) reveal task- and scale-dependent effects.\nFuture work includes N:M structured sparsity and scaling to 70B+.\n",

{
  "recorded_role": "user",
  "parentUuid": "a6dac1c2-57f9-4940-8ee4-a911a809e435",
  "isSidechain": false,
  "promptId": "ccc5fe4b-96a1-45ba-8d88-61daacb9d316",
  "type": "user",
  "uuid": "44b78682-2052-4298-a25b-3c88abc49536",
  "toolUseResult": {
    "filePath": "/home/ubuntu/.agent-ml-research-idea_gen_0509_14/projects/distill_sparse_swiglu/paper/introduction.tex",
    "oldString": "\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
    "newString": "\\item Empirical evidence that per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines, demonstrating the necessity of output-aligned training for composable masks (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
    "originalFile": "\\section{Introduction}\n\nSwiGLU feed-forward networks~\\cite{shazeer2020glu} in modern LLMs exhibit high contextual sparsity: at any given token, a large fraction of FFN neurons contribute negligibly to the output.\nSwiGLU's gated activation makes prediction challenging: importance depends on both gate and up-projection values, a target that magnitude-based methods handle suboptimally.\nLearned sparsity predictors~\\cite{liu2023dejavu,gautam2026fastforward} exploit this property by training small networks to anticipate which neurons are important, but they universally rely on binary cross-entropy (BCE) loss, treating each neuron as an independent classification target.\nYet at inference, sparsity budgets are allocated globally: the TEAL (training-free activation sparsity;~\\citealp{liu2025teal}) top-$k$ mechanism ranks all neurons across all layers and retains only those above a model-wide threshold.\nThis mismatch between per-neuron training and global inference allocation has not been formally analyzed.\nWhile MaskLLM~\\cite{fang2024maskllm} applies KL-based mask optimization, it targets static N:M weight sparsity; activation sparsity requires masks that generalize to unseen inputs, vary per token, and coordinate across layers through global allocation.\n\nGradient analysis reveals a structural asymmetry: BCE's gradient $\\partial \\mathcal{L}_{\\text{BCE}} / \\partial s_j = \\sigma(s_j) - m_j^*$ depends only on neuron $j$, while under KL divergence, the gradient couples each neuron to all others through the downstream Jacobian (\\S\\ref{sec:method:gradient}).\nKL's coupled gradients discover neuron importance patterns that diverge 68\\% from magnitude-based masks (per-layer Jaccard 32\\%).\nA target-swap ablation confirms that these discovered mask targets are the proximate quality driver at inference: BCE trained on KL-derived targets reproduces KL quality (max $\\Delta$$\\leq$0.79pp at 50\\%, s42), while magnitude-trained BCE achieves 28.13$\\pm$0.20. Gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage.\n\nWe instantiate a lightweight predictor (75.9M params, $<$1\\% of base) trained with forward KL and Gumbel-Sigmoid relaxation~\\cite{jang2017gumbel} on LLaMA-3.1-8B~\\cite{grattafiori2024llama3}.\nAt 30\\% sparsity under uniform allocation, the predictor reduces TEAL's perplexity by 14.5\\% at matched sparsity (8.81 vs.\\ 10.30 at 28.5\\%) with stable downstream (MMLU 40.7$\\pm$1.3\\%, 3-seed); global top-$k$ amplifies cross-seed variance ($7.3\\times$).\nAt 50\\%, the perplexity advantage widens (50\\% over TEAL), but downstream reasoning collapses regardless of method.\n\nPreliminary single-seed cross-architecture evaluation on Mistral-7B-v0.3~\\cite{jiang2023mistral} and Qwen-2.5-14B~\\cite{qwen2024qwen25} reveals consistent PPL improvements but scale-dependent downstream effects: Mistral shows 5/6 task wins; at 14B, PPL generalizes but reasoning declines (GSM8K $-$20pp, MMLU $-$2.8pp).\nThis paper is primarily an empirical study: we do not propose a new architecture or algorithm, but rather characterize how the training objective shapes sparsity prediction quality, and analyze interactions between allocation strategy, sparsity level, and model scale. Wall-clock speedup requires sparse kernel support not addressed here (Appendix~\\ref{app:latency}).\n\nOur contributions are:\n\\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]\n\\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).\n\\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).\n\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).\n\\item Preliminary analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).\n\\end{itemize}\n",
    "structuredPatch": [
      {
        "oldStart": 22,
        "oldLines": 6,
        "newStart": 22,
        "newLines": 6,
        "lines": [
          " \\begin{itemize}[nosep,leftmargin=*]",
          " \\item A target-swap ablation confirms that KL's discovered mask targets drive inference-stage quality; gradient coupling remains necessary at the discovery stage (\\S\\ref{sec:mask_analysis}).",
          " \\item These mask patterns render post-hoc compensation (+67.2M params) redundant at both tested sparsity levels (\\S\\ref{sec:isocompute}).",
          "-\\item Per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines; only end-to-end KL discovers composable masks, empirically showing that end-to-end output alignment, rather than coupling alone, determines mask quality (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
          "+\\item Empirical evidence that per-layer coupling (MSE) degrades below training-free baselines, demonstrating the necessity of output-aligned training for composable masks (\\S\\ref{sec:method:taxonomy}).",
          " \\item Preliminary analysis of allocation-to-downstream interactions at 7--14B: non-monotonic allocation preference at 8B and scale-dependent effects at 14B (\\S\\ref{sec:scaling}).",
          " \\end{itemize}"
        ]
      }
    ],
